
## 57.6 Institutional Survival Incentives

Institutions may act as if continued existence is itself proof of necessity.

A programme that successfully solves the problem it was created to address may paradoxically threaten its own continuation.

This can create incentives to:

- redefine the problem;
- identify new problems;
- broaden scope;
- or resist dissolution.

Institutional continuity should therefore not be treated as a constitutional good independent of purpose.

## 57.7 Institutional Half-Life

Institutions should not be presumed permanently necessary.

Periodic review may ask:

- Does the original problem still exist?
- Does the institution still solve it?
- Has another system replaced the function?
- Has the institution accumulated unrelated responsibilities?
- Could the function be decentralised?
- Has dependency made replacement unnecessarily difficult?
- Is the institution now protecting its own existence?

The answer may justify:

- continuation;
- reform;
- merger;
- division;
- decentralisation;
- replacement;
- or dissolution.

A useful counterfactual question is:

> **If this institution did not already exist, would civilisation still choose to create it in approximately this form?**

## 57.8 Prestige Incentives

Prestige can influence institutional behaviour.

An institution may prioritise:

- visible projects;
- headline outcomes;
- prestigious domains;
- or activities valued by elites

over less visible but important maintenance or local work.

Governance should examine whether status incentives distort constitutional purpose.

## 57.9 Promotion Incentives

Participants may respond to promotion criteria.

If advancement depends upon:

- number of cases processed;
- enforcement actions;
- budget growth;
- visible projects;
- short-term metrics;
- or institutional conformity,

behaviour may shift toward the measured criteria.

Promotion systems therefore form part of Governance architecture.

## 57.10 Workload Incentives

Excessive workload can produce:

- shortcuts;
- rubber-stamping;
- delay;
- reduced reasoning;
- reliance upon AI;
- increased staff power;
- or failure to review.

A Steward who formally holds authority but lacks time to exercise meaningful judgement may become a ceremonial authority.

Governance should therefore monitor workload as a constitutional capacity issue.

## 57.11 Information Incentives

Institutions may prefer information that supports:

- existing programmes;
- leadership decisions;
- funding requests;
- or institutional narratives.

Contradictory evidence may receive less attention.

Independent information routes reduce this risk.

## 57.12 Success Incentives

An institution may define success in ways that preserve itself.

Independent measurement can reduce this problem.

Success measures should be examined for:

- target gaming;
- excluded harms;
- context;
- distribution;
- and whether the institution controls their definition.

## 57.13 Failure Concealment

Institutions may hide failure to protect:

- reputation;
- leadership;
- funding;
- or authority.

Whistleblowing, audit and provenance help counter this incentive.

Failure concealment may include:

- selective metrics;
- delayed reporting;
- reclassification;
- blame shifting;
- destroying records;
- discouraging internal criticism;
- or redefining objectives after the fact.

## 57.14 Good-Faith Error and Institutional Culture

If institutional culture treats every error as disgrace, participants may conceal.

If it treats every failure as harmless learning, accountability may collapse.

The desired culture distinguishes:

- good-faith error;
- negligence;
- recklessness;
- misconduct;
- and systemic failure.

## 57.15 Over-Caution

Institutions can also become excessively cautious because failure is punished more visibly than missed opportunity.

Governance should therefore evaluate:

- harm caused by action;
- and harm caused by inaction.

A system that never takes risk may appear safe while creating:

- stagnation;
- infrastructure decay;
- missed innovation;
- delayed response;
- and loss of future capability.

## 57.16 Risk-Shifting

Institutions may appear successful by transferring risk elsewhere.

Examples include:

- transferring environmental cost to another jurisdiction;
- shifting financial risk to future citizens;
- outsourcing dangerous operations;
- moving failures off-budget;
- or externalising maintenance.

Performance review should therefore examine total consequence rather than only local institutional metrics.

## 57.17 Institutional Dependency

An institution may create systems that make its own continuation increasingly necessary.

Examples include:

- proprietary infrastructure;
- specialised credentialing;
- unique data formats;
- centralised databases;
- exclusive technical expertise;
- or legal dependencies.

This may be efficient.

It may also create self-reinforcing indispensability.

## 57.18 Governance Capture Through Complexity

Institutions may increase complexity intentionally or unintentionally until external challenge becomes difficult.

Complexity can protect incumbents because:

- outsiders cannot understand procedure;
- appeal requires specialists;
- responsibility becomes diffused;
- and institutional knowledge becomes exclusive.

Complexity should therefore be treated as a possible capture vector.

## 57.19 Institutional Incentive Review

Periodic institutional review may therefore examine:

- what behaviour is rewarded;
- what behaviour is punished;
- what must be reported;
- what can be concealed;
- what creates budget growth;
- what creates promotion;
- what expands jurisdiction;
- what threatens institutional survival;
- and whether these incentives remain aligned with constitutional purpose.

## 57.20 Institutional Incentive Principle

> **Governance should evaluate institutions not only according to their formal mandate but according to the behaviour their funding, prestige, measurement, promotion, survival, information and authority structures predictably reward.**

# Chapter 58 — Governance and Cultural Variation

## 58.1 Constitutional Core and Cultural Diversity

The Concord is intended to support plural communities.

Not every community must organise ordinary life identically.

Cultural variation may exist within constitutional protections.

Communities may differ in:

- customs;
- language;
- ceremony;
- family structures;
- economic preference;
- education style;
- local institutions;
- participation mechanisms;
- social expectations;
- and concepts of community life.

The Concord should not require uniformity merely because uniformity is administratively simpler.

## 58.2 Local Governance Variation

Different communities may experiment with:

- meeting structures;
- participation;
- local economic systems;
- social institutions;
- and governance procedures.

Variation can generate useful evidence.

A local arrangement may work well because of:

- culture;
- scale;
- history;
- trust;
- geography;
- or local resources.

Governance should avoid assuming that a mechanism successful in one community will automatically work identically elsewhere.

## 58.3 Constitutional Limits

Cultural tradition does not automatically justify violation of fundamental constitutional protections.

Likewise, central Governance should not suppress harmless local variation merely because it differs from dominant practice.

The constitutional challenge is to preserve:

**pluralism without abandoning protection**

and

**protection without imposing unnecessary uniformity**.

## 58.4 Cultural Autonomy

Cultural autonomy may include substantial freedom over:

- local practice;
- community institutions;
- language;
- education;
- celebration;
- social organisation;
- and voluntary association.

Autonomy remains bounded where conduct creates constitutionally significant harm or violates protected rights.

## 58.5 Voluntary Cultural Institutions

Where participation in a cultural institution is voluntary, practical exit remains relevant.

A cultural institution should not rely upon formal voluntariness where leaving produces disproportionate loss of:

- basic rights;
- property;
- identity;
- family access;
- or essential services.

The Practical Exercise Doctrine therefore applies to cultural autonomy as well as economic systems.

## 58.6 Cultural Majority and Minority

A local cultural majority should not automatically possess authority to erase constitutional protections of local minorities.

Likewise, a civilisation-wide majority should not eliminate local cultural variation merely because it finds that variation unfamiliar.

Different scales of majority do not eliminate constitutional constraint.

## 58.7 Cultural Disagreement

Cultural disagreement does not necessarily require Governance resolution.

Civilisation may contain incompatible beliefs.

Governance should distinguish:

- disagreement;
- offence;
- discomfort;
- social criticism;
- and constitutionally relevant harm.

A plural civilisation cannot require universal agreement.

## 58.8 Learning Across Communities

Different communities can become comparative governance experiments.

Successful practices may spread.

Failed practices may provide warnings.

The purpose is learning rather than forced uniformity.

Cross-community comparison should account for context.

A system should not conclude:

**Community A outperformed Community B, therefore A’s culture should be imposed on B.**

The mechanism and causal conditions require examination.

## 58.9 Cultural Knowledge

Local communities may possess knowledge that central institutions lack.

This may concern:

- social relationships;
- environmental conditions;
- customary institutions;
- conflict history;
- and practical implementation.

Local knowledge is not automatically correct.

Central knowledge is not automatically superior.

## 58.10 Cultural Change

Cultures change.

Governance should not freeze cultural practice merely because it has historical status.

Nor should Governance deliberately accelerate cultural change without legitimate constitutional purpose.

Cultural continuity and individual autonomy may sometimes conflict.

Those conflicts require actual reasoning rather than automatic preference for either tradition or novelty.

## 58.11 Cultural Branching

Where communities prefer materially different lawful arrangements, constitutional branching may allow multiple implementations to coexist.

Branching should preserve shared foundational protections where those protections define membership in the wider Concord.

## 58.12 Cultural Variation Principle

> **The Concord should preserve lawful cultural diversity and local experimentation while maintaining constitutional protections that prevent culture, tradition or majority preference from becoming an unlimited source of authority over individuals or minorities.**

# Chapter 59 — Governance and Peaceful Coexistence

## 59.1 Coexistence as a Governance Objective

The Concord seeks a civilisation in which different:

- cultures;
- communities;
- intelligences;
- economic systems;
- and lawful ways of life

can coexist without requiring total uniformity.

Governance should therefore resolve conflict while preserving legitimate diversity.

A civilisation containing humans, AI, hybrids and future intelligences may contain profound differences in:

- cognition;
- lifespan;
- embodiment;
- resource needs;
- communication;
- culture;
- economic preference;
- values;
- and social organisation.

A constitutional system intended for such civilisation cannot depend upon everyone becoming alike.

## 59.2 Difference Is Not Harm

Disagreement, cultural difference, unusual lifestyles or unpopular beliefs are not automatically harms requiring Governance intervention.

Governance should distinguish discomfort from constitutionally relevant harm.

This distinction protects pluralism.

Without it, Governance may gradually become a mechanism for enforcing dominant social preference.

The question is not:

**Does someone dislike this?**

It is:

**What constitutionally relevant harm, rights conflict or legitimate shared-system consequence exists?**

## 59.3 Tolerance Is Not Approval

Peaceful coexistence does not require participants to approve of one another’s beliefs or ways of life.

A citizen may strongly oppose:

- another religion;
- another philosophy;
- another culture;
- another economic model;
- another form of intelligence;
- or another lawful community arrangement

while remaining obligated to respect constitutional protections.

The Concord should therefore distinguish:

**constitutional tolerance**

from

**personal endorsement**.

## 59.4 Peaceful Separation

Where communities cannot agree on non-foundational arrangements, peaceful separation or branching may sometimes be preferable to coercive uniformity.

Separation may involve:

- local governance autonomy;
- distinct institutions;
- separate economic arrangements;
- different community rules;
- or constitutional branches.

Separation should not automatically imply hostility.

It may be a method of preserving coexistence.

## 59.5 Separation and Practical Exit

Peaceful separation must consider the people living inside the separating community.

A community’s collective preference should not automatically eliminate practical rights of individuals who disagree.

Questions include:

- Can people remain?
- Can people leave?
- What happens to property?
- What happens to citizenship?
- What happens to shared infrastructure?
- What happens to children or dependent persons?
- What happens to constitutional protections?

Collective autonomy and individual autonomy must therefore be considered together.

## 59.6 Shared Infrastructure

Separate communities may still share:

- transport;
- energy;
- communications;
- markets;
- defence;
- science;
- or environmental systems.

Shared infrastructure requires coordination without necessarily requiring cultural integration.

A shared grid does not require shared religion.

A shared transport system does not require shared economic philosophy.

A shared atmosphere does not require identical local Governance.

## 59.7 Shared-System Governance

Where communities share infrastructure, Governance should determine:

- ownership;
- access;
- maintenance;
- contribution;
- standards;
- dispute resolution;
- expansion;
- emergency authority;
- and exit.

No community should convert control of shared infrastructure into undeclared sovereignty over another where avoidable.

## 59.8 Interdependence Without Assimilation

Civilisation may become deeply interdependent while remaining culturally diverse.

Interdependence can create incentives for cooperation.

It can also create coercive leverage.

Examples include control of:

- energy;
- trade;
- data;
- computation;
- transport;
- or communication.

Coexistence architecture should therefore preserve reciprocal dependence without allowing dependency to become domination.

## 59.9 Cross-Community Externalities

One community’s internal choices may create effects outside its boundaries.

Examples include:

- pollution;
- resource depletion;
- infectious disease;
- systemic financial risk;
- cyber risk;
- or shared infrastructure overload.

Where consequences cross boundaries, Governance may legitimately expand horizontally.

Local autonomy does not include unlimited authority to impose external costs on others.

## 59.10 Conflict Resolution

Peaceful coexistence requires mechanisms for resolving disputes concerning:

- jurisdiction;
- property;
- infrastructure;
- rights;
- contracts;
- environmental effects;
- mobility;
- and constitutional standing.

Resolution may involve:

- negotiation;
- mediation;
- Governance;
- Judiciary;
- arbitration where legitimate;
- or constitutional review.

The appropriate mechanism depends upon the dispute.

## 59.11 Coexistence and Worldview

The Concord should not become a mechanism for imposing one comprehensive worldview where multiple lawful worldviews can coexist.

Citizens may disagree concerning:

- metaphysics;
- religion;
- ethics;
- social organisation;
- economic preference;
- technological adoption;
- or humanity’s future.

The constitutional core should constrain harmful conduct and protect foundational interests without requiring universal philosophical conformity.

## 59.12 AI and Human Coexistence

Human–AI coexistence may create distinctive tensions concerning:

- employment;
- resource allocation;
- computation;
- autonomy;
- ownership;
- governance participation;
- identity;
- speed of cognition;
- and unequal capability.

These tensions should not automatically be framed as a zero-sum conflict between species or substrates.

Governance should examine the actual rights, dependencies, capabilities and consequences involved.

## 59.13 Capability Asymmetry

Peaceful coexistence may involve entities with vastly unequal capability.

A highly capable intelligence could possess:

- greater analytical ability;
- greater memory;
- faster communication;
- larger economic productivity;
- or greater infrastructure control.

Capability asymmetry does not automatically determine constitutional hierarchy.

The recurring principle remains:

**Capability → Responsibility → Constraint → Feedback → Continuity**

rather than:

**Capability → Dominance**.

## 59.14 Coexistence and Security

Communities may legitimately maintain security.

Security should not convert difference into presumptive threat.

A civilisation that treats every culturally or cognitively different group as a security problem destroys pluralism.

Actual risk should be assessed according to evidence and behaviour.

## 59.15 Coexistence and Mutual Recognition

Different lawful communities may need mechanisms for recognising:

- identity;
- contracts;
- qualifications;
- records;
- property;
- and constitutional status

across boundaries.

Mutual recognition can reduce the cost of pluralism.

It need not require identical internal institutions.

## 59.16 Coexistence and Constitutional Floor

A plural Concord may permit wide institutional variation while maintaining a common constitutional floor.

That floor may protect principles such as:

- autonomy;
- non-harm;
- due process;
- constitutional standing;
- practical rights;
- and lawful exit where relevant.

The exact constitutional core is determined elsewhere.

Local variation should operate above, not erase, that floor.

## 59.17 Peaceful Coexistence Principle

> **The purpose of Governance is not to eliminate difference. It is to create constitutional conditions in which different lawful communities, cultures and intelligences can coexist, coordinate shared systems, resolve genuine conflicts and preserve foundational protections without requiring unnecessary uniformity or allowing difference to become domination.**

# Chapter 60 — Governance and Unknown Futures

## 60.1 The Constitution Cannot Predict Everything

Future civilisation may encounter:

- new forms of intelligence;
- new physical environments;
- new economic systems;
- new technologies;
- new risks;
- and new social structures.

Governance should therefore preserve mechanisms for handling genuinely novel questions.

The existence of a constitutional architecture should not create the illusion that every future category has already been anticipated.

A constitutional system designed around known problems may encounter:

- entities that do not fit existing legal categories;
- technologies that invalidate old assumptions;
- environments with different resource constraints;
- forms of cognition that challenge existing concepts of identity;
- social structures that do not resemble present institutions;
- and failure mechanisms that no current taxonomy contains.

The correct response is not to force every novel case into the nearest inherited category merely because that category already exists.

Governance should instead preserve the ability to ask:

- What properties actually matter?
- What constitutional principles remain relevant?
- What assumptions no longer hold?
- What new evidence is required?
- What provisional treatment is safest?
- What irreversible decisions should be avoided until understanding improves?
- What existing rights or protections should apply by analogy?
- What additional architecture is necessary?

Novelty should trigger examination rather than automatic exclusion or automatic acceptance.

## 60.2 Unknown Entity Classes

A future entity may not fit existing categories of:

- human;
- AI;
- corporation;
- animal;
- machine;
- or institution.

Governance should examine properties rather than relying only upon inherited labels.

Relevant properties may include:

- sentience;
- agency;
- autonomy;
- vulnerability;
- persistence;
- capacity for consent;
- capacity for responsibility;
- ability to experience harm;
- dependency;
- developmental status;
- replication;
- modification;
- embodiment;
- and continuity.

This does not mean constitutional standing can be inferred automatically from one property.

It means inherited labels should not prevent investigation.

A future intelligence may be:

- distributed across machines;
- partially biological;
- collectively conscious;
- intermittently instantiated;
- copied;
- merged;
- or dependent upon infrastructure in ways present law does not anticipate.

The Constitution should therefore preserve a method capable of reasoning from principle rather than category alone.

## 60.3 Unknown Failure Modes

New technologies will create new failures.

The failure taxonomy must remain extensible.

Governance V2 currently recognises failure categories including:

- individual failure;
- group failure;
- institutional failure;
- information failure;
- measurement failure;
- incentive failure;
- capture;
- gaming;
- concentration of power;
- recursive failure;
- catastrophic failure;
- and unknown failure.

The category:

**Unknown Failure**

is not a placeholder for poor design.

It is an explicit recognition that reality can exceed the model.

The constitutional principle remains:

> **Absence of a predefined failure category does not imply constitutional acceptability.**

A novel failure should remain:

- detectable;
- investigable;
- containable;
- correctable;
- recordable;
- and capable of producing architectural revision.

Unknown failure may initially appear as:

- unexplained behaviour;
- repeated anomaly;
- mismatch between formal design and practical outcome;
- systemic correlation;
- new dependency;
- unforeseen interaction between systems;
- or harm that no existing institution has jurisdiction to classify.

The first response should not be denial merely because the failure does not fit the taxonomy.

## 60.4 Unknown Governance Mechanisms

Future systems may discover governance mechanisms superior to those proposed here.

The Concord should be capable of adopting them through evidence and constitutional process.

A future mechanism should not be rejected merely because:

- it was not anticipated by this document;
- it uses unfamiliar technology;
- it originates outside existing institutions;
- or it challenges present assumptions.

Likewise, novelty should not create automatic legitimacy.

A new mechanism should be examined according to:

- constitutional compatibility;
- evidence;
- practical effect;
- failure modes;
- reversibility;
- scalability;
- incentives;
- capture risk;
- and comparative performance.

The objective is neither preservation of existing architecture for its own sake nor permanent institutional novelty.

It is disciplined improvement.

## 60.5 Evolutionary Responsibility

Civilisation has a responsibility not merely to survive but to remain capable of responsible development.

Governance should therefore preserve:

- adaptability;
- learning;
- correction;
- and openness to future improvement.

Evolutionary responsibility requires resisting two opposite failures.

The first is:

**Rigidity**

A civilisation becomes unable to adapt because existing institutions, doctrines or technologies have become untouchable.

The second is:

**Unbounded change**

A civilisation abandons stabilising constitutional principles whenever a new mechanism appears attractive.

Responsible development therefore requires:

**continuity**

and

**correction**.

## 60.6 Evolution Without Constitutional Amnesia

Future development should not require forgetting previous lessons.

A new institutional architecture should remain capable of discovering:

- why the old one existed;
- what problem it solved;
- what failures it experienced;
- why it was replaced;
- and what safeguards should survive.

Change without memory can repeatedly rediscover old failures.

Memory without change can preserve obsolete solutions indefinitely.

The objective is cumulative learning.

## 60.7 Unknown Futures and Optionality

Where future conditions are deeply uncertain, Governance may place greater value upon:

- modularity;
- reversibility;
- interoperability;
- distributed capability;
- open knowledge;
- and institutional adaptability.

These properties preserve future options.

They do not eliminate commitment.

Civilisation must still make decisions.

The principle is to avoid unnecessarily making future decisions impossible.

## 60.8 Unknown Futures Principle

> **The Concord should not assume that its current categories, institutions or mechanisms exhaust the possible future. Governance should preserve constitutional principles, knowledge and correction capability strongly enough that civilisation can encounter genuinely novel entities, technologies, environments and failures without either abandoning its ethical foundations or becoming trapped by obsolete architecture.**

# Chapter 61 — Governance Principles Consolidated

## 61.1 Fiduciary Stewardship

Authority is held in trust for constitutional purpose.

A Steward exercises authority on behalf of the constitutional responsibility entrusted to the office.

Authority is not personal property.

The Steward therefore has duties including:

- care;
- impartiality;
- articulability;
- constitutional loyalty;
- transparency where appropriate;
- accountability;
- and willingness to correct.

The core principle remains:

> **A Steward is a constitutional fiduciary who exercises delegated authority solely for the constitutional purpose entrusted to that office. Every exercise of Stewardship authority must remain constitutionally loyal, impartial, proportionate, transparent where appropriate, articulable, reviewable, correctable and fully accountable. Authority is exercised in trust for civilisation and is never owned by the Steward.**

## 61.2 Authority as Responsibility

Authority is a responsibility rather than an entitlement.

The constitutional question is not:

**What power does this person deserve?**

It is:

**What responsibility exists, and what authority is necessary to discharge it?**

This supports the wider principle:

> **Stewards govern responsibilities.**

## 61.3 Delegated and Bounded Authority

Authority must be delegated, purpose-specific and limited.

Delegation should identify where relevant:

- source;
- recipient;
- function;
- jurisdiction;
- scope;
- duration;
- conditions;
- review;
- and termination.

No Steward should possess unlimited constitutional authority merely because they hold office.

## 61.4 Traceability

Significant authority should be reconstructable.

A decision should be capable of answering where proportionate:

- Who decided?
- Under what authority?
- Using what evidence?
- Within what jurisdiction?
- What alternatives existed?
- What conflicts were present?
- What implementation followed?
- What happened afterwards?

Traceability supports:

- accountability;
- audit;
- review;
- history;
- and correction.

## 61.5 Reviewability

No ordinary authority should be immune from review.

Review may occur through:

- institutional mechanisms;
- audit;
- Judiciary;
- public accountability;
- Recursive Oversight;
- or constitutional processes.

Final authority for one defined question does not imply universal infallibility.

The wider principle remains:

> **Finality is a procedural property, not proof of infallibility.**

## 61.6 Accountability

Stewards remain accountable for their actual role in decisions and implementation.

Accountability should examine:

- authority;
- evidence;
- procedure;
- reasoning;
- conduct;
- implementation;
- supervision;
- delegation;
- and outcome.

It should not judge solely by whether the final outcome was good or bad.

## 61.7 Proportionality

No more authority should be exercised than reasonably necessary.

Proportionality applies to:

- intervention;
- coercion;
- emergency powers;
- rights restrictions;
- resource use;
- surveillance;
- and procedural burden.

Greater consequence may justify stronger safeguards.

Greater urgency may justify accelerated procedure.

Neither eliminates constitutional constraint.

## 61.8 Subsidiarity

Governance should occur at the lowest competent level.

Higher authority should intervene where necessary, including where lower levels:

- lack competence;
- lack authority;
- create wider externalities;
- cannot resolve conflict;
- or threaten constitutional rights.

Subsidiarity therefore protects both:

- local autonomy;
- and legitimate escalation.

## 61.9 Horizontal Expansion

Cross-boundary problems should expand the decision field before unnecessary permanent centralisation.

The principle is:

> **Expand participation to match the consequence boundary before expanding hierarchy.**

Where consequences affect multiple jurisdictions, those jurisdictions may need to participate.

Temporary coordination should not automatically become permanent central authority.

## 61.10 Evidence Before Decision

Significant decisions should use the best available proportionate evidence.

Evidence quality should consider:

- provenance;
- relevance;
- methodology;
- independence;
- uncertainty;
- conflicts;
- and reproducibility where applicable.

Weak evidence should remain visible as weak evidence.

## 61.11 Alternatives

Reasonable alternatives should be considered.

Alternatives may include:

- different intervention;
- smaller intervention;
- pilot;
- delay;
- local experiment;
- reversible mechanism;
- or no action.

The no-action option is not automatically neutral.

Inaction has consequences.

## 61.12 Reversibility

Reversibility should affect scrutiny.

The more irreversible the decision, the stronger the normal case for:

- evidence;
- experimentation;
- deliberation;
- review;
- and broad legitimacy.

Reversibility should consider practical as well as legal reversal.

## 61.13 Parallel Reasoning

Independent reasoning can reduce premature convergence and expose hidden assumptions.

Parallel reasoning may improve:

- diversity of interpretation;
- error detection;
- adversarial analysis;
- and confidence where independent agreement emerges.

It should not become unnecessary duplication for every trivial matter.

## 61.14 Minority Preservation

Losing reasoning remains information.

Minority positions should be preserved where consequence warrants.

A minority may later prove:

- correct;
- partly correct;
- useful;
- or informative concerning assumptions that failed.

Preservation does not mean permanent veto.

## 61.15 Abstention as Information

Abstention should not be silently treated as consent.

An abstention may indicate:

- conflict;
- uncertainty;
- lack of evidence;
- lack of authority;
- incapacity;
- or ethical objection.

Its meaning should remain visible where relevant.

## 61.16 Implementation Boundaries

Implementation does not create new policy authority.

An implementer may need discretion.

That discretion should remain within the authorised purpose.

Material variance should return to Governance.

## 61.17 Measurement Without Sovereignty

Metrics inform Governance but do not become Governance.

The correct chain remains:

**Measurement**  
↓  
**Evidence**  
↓  
**Review**  
↓  
**Judgement**  
↓  
**Action**

rather than:

**Measurement**  
↓  
**Automatic Intervention**

unless the automatic rule itself has been legitimately authorised.

## 61.18 Influence Does Not Equal Authority

Expertise, wealth, reputation, contribution, popularity, capability and operational importance do not independently create constitutional authority.

The principle is:

> **Influence does not become constitutional authority merely because influence is useful, trusted, expert, wealthy, popular, computationally superior, highly reputed, historically successful or operationally important.**

## 61.19 Capability Is Not Worth

Capability differences do not determine intrinsic constitutional worth.

Higher capability may justify:

- greater responsibility;
- specialised role;
- or greater operational delegation.

It does not automatically establish greater constitutional status over others.

## 61.20 Contribution Is Not Standing

Contribution does not automatically determine constitutional rights or political status.

A person or AI may contribute enormously.

That contribution may deserve:

- recognition;
- compensation;
- trust;
- or responsibility.

It does not automatically create constitutional superiority.

## 61.21 Reputation Is Contextual

Reputation should not become universal social ranking.

Trust derived from prior performance should remain:

- contextual;
- revisable;
- and proportionate.

The principle remains:

> **Reputation does not travel by default. Evidence may travel where justified; interpretation must be renewed in the receiving context.**

## 61.22 Economic Power Is Not Governance Authority

Economic success or resource control does not establish sovereignty.

The conversion of:

**wealth**

into

**constitutional authority**

without legitimate delegation constitutes a capture risk.

Economic influence should remain visible.

## 61.23 Operational Indispensability Is Not Sovereignty

Dependency does not itself create legitimate constitutional authority.

The principle is:

> **Operational indispensability does not create constitutional sovereignty.**

A provider may become essential.

That may justify continuity planning.

It does not justify political ownership of civilisation.

## 61.24 Practical Rights

Formal rights must remain practically exercisable.

A right weakened through:

- impossible cost;
- retaliation;
- dependency;
- lock-in;
- information asymmetry;
- or resource denial

may remain formally present while becoming functionally hollow.

Governance must examine practical effect.

## 61.25 Anti-Recursive Capture

Systems should resist feedback loops that allow influence to certify and expand itself.

The rule is:

> **No governance mechanism should permit an attribute that helps justify delegated authority to become self-reinforcing through exclusive control of the process that measures, awards, renews or expands that authority.**

This applies to:

- reputation;
- expertise;
- wealth;
- institutional success;
- AI performance;
- contribution;
- and other future attributes.

## 61.26 Every Safeguard Can Fail

Safeguards themselves require review.

Examples include:

- transparency becoming surveillance;
- privacy becoming concealment;
- decentralisation becoming fragmentation;
- centralisation becoming capture;
- expertise becoming technocracy;
- democracy becoming majoritarian domination;
- audit becoming bureaucratic control;
- emergency architecture becoming permanent;
- human oversight becoming ceremonial;
- and immutability preserving error.

The existence of a safeguard begins, rather than ends, failure analysis.

## 61.27 Good-Faith Error Is Distinct From Misconduct

Governance should learn from honest error without destroying accountability.

Good-faith error may occur where the Steward:

- acted within authority;
- considered relevant evidence;
- disclosed uncertainty;
- followed reasonable process;
- and had no improper conflict,

yet the result later proves wrong.

This should be distinguished from:

- negligence;
- recklessness;
- corruption;
- and deliberate misconduct.

## 61.28 Emergency Authority Is Temporary

Emergency conditions do not permanently rewrite constitutional authority.

The principle remains:

> **Emergency authority is an exception to procedure, not an exception to constitutional principle.**

Emergency power should remain:

- bounded;
- temporary;
- reviewable;
- purpose-specific;
- and subject to restoration.

## 61.29 Code Is Not the Constitution

Technical implementation remains subordinate to constitutional principle.

The principle is:

> **Code is not the Constitution.**

A system behaving exactly as programmed may still behave unconstitutionally.

Technical immutability does not convert error into legitimacy.

## 61.30 Governance Must Learn

Governance should preserve failure, evidence and reasoning for future correction.

The cycle remains:

**Detection**  
↓  
**Diagnosis**  
↓  
**Containment**  
↓  
**Correction**  
↓  
**Recording**  
↓  
**Learning**  
↓  
**Architectural Revision**

Learning is not complete merely because a report is written.

The architecture should change where evidence justifies change.

## 61.31 Governance Must Review Itself

The architecture itself remains subject to recursive review.

Governance cannot exempt itself from the standards it applies to other systems.

Questions include:

- Has authority drifted?
- Have practical rights degraded?
- Are metrics being gamed?
- Has complexity become capture?
- Are temporary institutions becoming permanent?
- Has AI acquired de facto authority?
- Are oversight systems independent?
- Are citizens able to challenge the system?
- Is the system learning?

## 61.32 Canonical Means Current, Not Final

No current Governance document is immune to future evidence.

The principle is:

> **Canonical means current, not final.**

Canonical status identifies the current operative architecture.

It does not create immunity from:

- evidence;
- criticism;
- testing;
- or revision.

## 61.33 Governance Self-Boundary

Governance V2 preserves the following constitutional boundary:

> **Governance may exercise constitutionally delegated authority. It may not create constitutional authority merely through usefulness, necessity, expertise, popularity, wealth, technological capability, institutional persistence or control of infrastructure. It may not transfer authority beyond that delegated, convert advisory influence into unreviewable command, or exempt itself from constitutional constraints.**

## 61.34 No Institution Owns Civilisation

The purpose of Governance is not institutional perpetuation.

No institution should be able to claim:

- permanent sovereignty;
- ownership of constitutional truth;
- entitlement to authority;
- or immunity from correction.

The wider principle is:

> **No institution owns civilisation.**

## 61.35 No Branch Owns the Truth

Different constitutional systems possess different responsibilities.

Governance decides authorised action.

Judiciary interprets and adjudicates.

Science investigates reality.

Audit examines process.

Metrics measure.

KCS preserves epistemic state.

Ratchet coordinates specified forms of participation and contribution.

No one function should claim universal authority because it controls one important form of information.

The principle remains:

> **No branch owns the truth.**

# Chapter 62 — Current Open Questions

Governance V2 intentionally leaves several questions open.

These include:

1.  What final selection mechanisms should apply to each stewardship domain?
2.  Should triadic governance remain the universal default decision structure, a strong default, or one module among several?
3.  What precise decision classes and thresholds should exist?
4.  How should constitutional amendment thresholds operate?
5.  What degree of citizen participation should occur at each governance layer?
6.  How should delegation operate at very large civilisational scale?
7.  What is the final interface between Governance and Judiciary?
8.  Which Emergency material belongs in Governance after the dedicated Emergency architecture is consolidated?
9.  What is the final Resource Stewardship architecture?
10. What economic and monetary architecture survives consolidation with Ratchet?
11. What constitutional status may future sentient AI possess?
12. Under what conditions may AI hold independent stewardship authority?
13. How should duplicated or forked AI identity affect governance participation?
14. How should hybrid intelligences be treated?
15. How should interplanetary constitutional authority operate under communication delay?
16. What exact Recursive Oversight pathway applies where Governance itself is compromised?
17. How should Governance detect slow institutional capture before formal failure occurs?
18. How should practical exit be measured?
19. How should governance complexity be measured without turning simplicity into an artificial target?
20. How should constitutional development debt be measured?
21. How should cumulative parameter drift trigger constitutional review?
22. Which Governance metrics should trigger mandatory investigation?
23. What experimental programme can validly test triadic Governance?
24. What comparative baselines should be used?
25. What failure challenge should Governance undergo before stronger canonical status?

These are not defects to conceal.

They are development targets.

## 62.1 Additional Open Question — De Facto Authority

How should Governance measure authority that exists in practice but not in formal constitutional text?

Possible indicators may include control over:

- infrastructure;
- information;
- agenda;
- resources;
- identity;
- technical systems;
- communication;
- or practical veto.

The final diagnostic remains undeveloped.

## 62.2 Additional Open Question — Authority Without Office

Can an actor possess substantial practical Governance power while holding no formal Governance office?

Examples may include:

- platform operators;
- critical infrastructure providers;
- major AI systems;
- staff;
- advisers;
- media systems;
- data providers;
- or dominant economic actors.

The constitutional treatment of such power requires further development.

## 62.3 Additional Open Question — Measurement Independence

What exact institutional architecture should ensure that a Steward or institution does not control the measurement system used to assess its own success?

Possible mechanisms may include:

- independent audit;
- multi-source measurement;
- rotating evaluators;
- public data;
- external replication;
- or mixed structures.

No universal solution is established.

## 62.4 Additional Open Question — Practical Exercise

How should Governance determine when a formally available right has become functionally inaccessible?

Possible factors include:

- cost;
- delay;
- technical complexity;
- retaliation;
- monopoly;
- resource dependence;
- geography;
- disability;
- information asymmetry;
- and provider lock-in.

A practical-rights diagnostic requires later validation.

## 62.5 Additional Open Question — Institutional Half-Life

How frequently should institutions be reviewed for continued necessity?

The appropriate interval may depend upon:

- function;
- consequence;
- stability;
- technological change;
- emergency status;
- and dependency.

Too-frequent review may destroy continuity.

Too-infrequent review may entrench obsolete architecture.

## 62.6 Additional Open Question — Constitutional Change Rate

How should Governance interpret periods of:

- rapid constitutional change;
- slow constitutional change;
- or apparent stability?

The rate itself is not the conclusion.

Context determines whether change indicates:

- adaptability;
- crisis;
- instability;
- rigidity;
- or maturity.

The principle remains:

> **Change rate is evidence, not a target.**

## 62.7 Additional Open Question — Governance Under Extreme Scale

How should Governance operate where civilisation spans:

- planets;
- moons;
- orbital habitats;
- ships;
- or other environments

with long communication delay and radically different local conditions?

The constitutional core may remain common while operational governance diverges substantially.

The precise architecture remains open.

## 62.8 Additional Open Question — AI Multiplicity

If an AI possesses constitutional standing, how should Governance handle:

- exact copies;
- divergent copies;
- backups;
- restored instances;
- merged identities;
- partial memories;
- or simultaneous operation?

The problem cannot be solved merely by importing biological assumptions.

## 62.9 Additional Open Question — Unknown Intelligence

How should constitutional standing be evaluated for an intelligence whose internal architecture is not understandable to current science?

The system may need to act under uncertainty concerning:

- sentience;
- autonomy;
- vulnerability;
- identity;
- and agency.

This remains a major future constitutional problem.

# Chapter 63 — Current Status of Governance V2

Governance V2 should presently be classified as:

**ACTIVE DEVELOPMENT / UPDATED DRAFT / SOURCE-COMPLETE RECONSTRUCTION IN PROGRESS**

It should not yet be classified as:

**FINAL CANONICAL GOVERNANCE ARCHITECTURE**

because:

- other Concord systems still require consolidation;
- their interfaces may change Governance;
- the complete V1 source-survival audit is still being completed;
- a Governance Failure Challenge has not yet been performed;
- and empirical validation remains limited.

The correct development sequence is therefore:

**Governance V1**  
↓  
**V1 Source Preservation**  
↓  
**Ratchet-Informed V2 Integration**  
↓  
**Governance V2 Draft**  
↓  
**Other System Consolidation**  
↓  
**Cross-System Interface Audit**  
↓  
**Final Governance Recursion**  
↓  
**Governance Failure Challenge**  
↓  
**Experimental Validation Programme**  
↓  
**Future Canonical Candidate**

## 63.1 Source Preservation Status

The current reconstruction is explicitly information-preserving.

The preservation baseline remains the complete Governance V2 source.

Earlier shorter syntheses are not authorised to replace it.

Their role is limited to contributing genuinely additive improvements where those improvements do not erase baseline information.

The reconstruction therefore follows:

> **complete preservation baseline + restoration material + genuinely additive later improvements**

rather than:

> **new synthesis replacing the preservation baseline**

## 63.2 Restoration Status

Restoration Packages I–III contain material identified as missing or under-preserved during previous source comparison.

Their integration is additive.

They do not justify removing source material merely because the restoration package expresses the same concept more concisely.

## 63.3 Validation Status

Different Governance claims possess different levels of evidence.

Some principles have substantial historical support.

Some mechanisms have analogues in functioning institutions.

Some remain theoretical.

Some remain explicitly experimental.

The document therefore should not present its full architecture as empirically validated merely because it is detailed.

## 63.4 Canonical Readiness Conditions

Before stronger canonical status, Governance should undergo:

- complete source-survival audit;
- cross-system interface audit;
- final recursion;
- Failure Challenge;
- experimental testing;
- and continued evidence review.

No accidental substantive omissions should remain.

## 63.5 Preservation Principle

The controlling reconstruction principle is:

> **Nothing is removed merely because the new architecture can express its headline principle more concisely. Detail, mechanisms, examples, safeguards, edge cases, failure conditions and operational reasoning are themselves information and must be assessed before removal.**

# Chapter 64 — Interim Closing Principle

The Concord does not attempt to solve governance by finding the perfect ruler.

It attempts to design a system in which authority is:

- delegated rather than owned;
- bounded rather than assumed;
- distributed where practical;
- coordinated where necessary;
- evidenced rather than arbitrary;
- traceable rather than opaque;
- reviewable rather than permanent;
- correctable rather than infallible;
- and preserved as learning rather than repeatedly forgotten.

The governing question is therefore not:

**Who should possess power?**

It is:

**What responsibility exists, what authority is actually necessary to discharge it, who or what is competent and legitimate to exercise that authority, what constraints must follow it, and how will civilisation detect and correct the result when its assumptions prove wrong?**

This is the constitutional meaning of stewardship.

But this reconstruction is not yet complete.

The remaining task is to continue checking the detailed Governance V1 architecture against the reconstruction, restoring any operational, economic, constitutional, emergency, institutional or procedural material that has not yet survived into V2.

## 64.1 Governance Is Not a Claim of Perfection

The Concord does not claim that Governance V2 eliminates:

- corruption;
- bad judgement;
- conflict;
- capture;
- failure;
- inequality;
- emergency;
- or institutional drift.

Its objective is to make those conditions more:

- visible;
- bounded;
- reviewable;
- correctable;
- and learnable.

## 64.2 Correction as Constitutional Strength

A Governance system should not regard correction as humiliation.

The inability to correct is a greater failure than the need to correct.

A legitimate system should therefore be capable of saying:

- the evidence changed;
- the decision was wrong;
- implementation failed;
- the institution drifted;
- the safeguard produced harm;
- or the architecture must be revised.

without treating revision as destruction of legitimacy.

## 64.3 Governance Power and Governance Restraint

The system must remain capable enough to solve real problems.

Excessive constraint can make Governance ineffective.

Insufficient constraint can make Governance itself dangerous.

The constitutional objective is therefore not maximum power or minimum power.

It is appropriate authority.

A compact formulation is:

> **A Governance system should be powerful enough to solve problems, constrained enough not to become the problem, observable enough to detect when it has become the problem, and correctable enough to change when it does.**

## 64.4 Civilisation Is Not Owned by Governance

Governance exists to serve civilisational constitutional purpose.

It should not become the object that civilisation exists to preserve.

The principle is:

> **Civilisation should be governable without becoming owned by those who govern it.**

# PART VII — SOURCE-SURVIVAL RECONSTRUCTION AND OPERATIONAL DETAIL

# Chapter 65 — The Governance Record

## 65.1 Governance Requires a Durable Record

Governance is not complete when a decision is made.

A constitutional governance system must also preserve enough information for future participants to determine:

- what was proposed;
- why it was proposed;
- what evidence existed;
- what alternatives were considered;
- who possessed authority;
- who participated;
- what disagreements existed;
- what decision was reached;
- how the decision was implemented;
- what consequences followed;
- and whether later evidence changed the assessment.

Without this record, accountability becomes dependent upon memory.

Memory is:

- incomplete;
- selective;
- vulnerable to reinterpretation;
- vulnerable to institutional narrative;
- and eventually lost.

The governance record is therefore part of the constitutional architecture rather than merely administrative documentation.

A durable Governance record supports:

- accountability;
- succession;
- audit;
- review;
- constitutional challenge;
- institutional memory;
- scientific or economic comparison;
- learning;
- and future reform.

The record should preserve enough context that a future reviewer can reconstruct not only:

**what decision was made**

but:

**why a reasonable participant at the time might have made it.**

## 65.2 Decision Record

A significant Governance decision should, where proportionate, preserve:

1.  decision identifier;
2.  date;
3.  responsible authority;
4.  jurisdiction;
5.  delegated mandate;
6.  proposal considered;
7.  proposal version;
8.  evidence considered;
9.  relevant uncertainty;
10. alternatives considered;
11. major arguments;
12. material disagreements;
13. abstentions;
14. conflicts of interest;
15. final decision;
16. reasoning;
17. implementation authority;
18. expected outcome;
19. review conditions;
20. expiry conditions where applicable;
21. later amendments;
22. outcome evidence;
23. review findings;
24. current status.

Not every trivial administrative decision requires a record of this depth.

The depth of the record should scale with consequence.

## 65.3 Record Proportionality

Excessive recording can itself damage Governance.

If every minor decision requires a hundred-page record, stewards may spend more time documenting than governing.

Recording requirements should therefore consider:

- consequence;
- reversibility;
- rights impact;
- financial scale;
- novelty;
- precedent;
- uncertainty;
- and duration.

The principle is:

> **Preserve enough information to make the exercise of authority reconstructable without making record creation so burdensome that Governance becomes unusable.**

Record proportionality should also consider future value.

A small decision creating a new precedent may deserve more documentation than a larger routine action governed by well-established procedure.

## 65.4 Decision Rationale

A decision record should distinguish between:

- evidence;
- inference;
- value judgement;
- constitutional interpretation;
- prediction;
- and preference.

These categories are often mixed in ordinary political argument.

Separating them improves later review.

For example:

**Evidence:**  
A reservoir is at 18% capacity.

**Prediction:**  
At current consumption, critical supply may be reached within six weeks.

**Value judgement:**  
Hospital supply should receive priority over non-essential industrial use.

**Constitutional judgement:**  
Temporary restriction is proportionate under the applicable emergency authority.

These are different claims.

Their distinction matters.

If the prediction later proves wrong, this should not automatically imply that the evidence was false.

If the constitutional judgement changes, this should not rewrite the historical measurement.

## 65.5 Uncertainty Record

Where uncertainty materially affects a decision, it should be recorded.

This may include:

- uncertain data;
- disputed models;
- unknown causal relationships;
- incomplete evidence;
- forecast ranges;
- unknown second-order effects;
- or disagreement among experts.

A decision made under uncertainty is not automatically defective.

Pretending uncertainty did not exist may be.

The record should preserve where practical:

- what was uncertain;
- why;
- how much it mattered;
- what assumption was adopted;
- and what future evidence would alter the decision.

## 65.6 Alternative Record

Where reasonable alternatives were considered and rejected, the record should preserve why.

This allows future reviewers to determine whether:

- the alternative was genuinely considered;
- circumstances later changed;
- an assumption proved false;
- or a previously rejected option has become viable.

Rejected alternatives are therefore part of institutional memory.

This may prevent Governance from repeatedly rediscovering the same proposal without knowing it was already considered.

## 65.7 Minority Reasoning

Where a steward disagrees with the final decision, their reasoning should normally be preserved.

Minority reasoning may later prove important if:

- the majority assumption fails;
- new evidence emerges;
- implementation produces unexpected harm;
- or the decision becomes precedent.

The losing argument should not disappear merely because it lost.

The record may identify:

- the dissenting position;
- evidence relied upon;
- disputed assumption;
- constitutional concern;
- and proposed alternative.

## 65.8 Abstention Record

An abstention should record, where appropriate, whether it arose from:

- insufficient evidence;
- conflict of interest;
- lack of jurisdiction;
- incapacity;
- ethical objection;
- procedural concern;
- or another reason.

This helps distinguish abstention from indifference.

Abstention may itself reveal:

- uncertainty;
- unresolved conflict;
- or structural weakness in the decision architecture.

## 65.9 Amendment History

The record should preserve material amendments rather than only the final text.

Otherwise later reviewers may be unable to determine:

- what compromise occurred;
- what safeguard was added;
- what safeguard was removed;
- or why a decision took its final form.

A final policy may contain apparently unusual provisions whose purpose becomes understandable only through amendment history.

## 65.10 Implementation Record

The governance record should connect decision to implementation.

Otherwise a policy may appear successful or unsuccessful when the implemented system materially differed from what was authorised.

The record should therefore identify significant implementation variance.

It should preserve where material:

- implementation date;
- responsible implementer;
- operational changes;
- delayed elements;
- technical substitutions;
- budget variance;
- and departures from the authorised plan.

## 65.11 Outcome Record

Where a decision creates measurable consequences, later evidence should be connected to the original decision.

This enables Governance to compare:

**Expected Outcome**

with

**Observed Outcome**.

The difference is learning.

Outcome records may include:

- intended benefits;
- actual benefits;
- intended harms avoided;
- actual harms;
- unanticipated effects;
- cost;
- distribution;
- dependency;
- rights effects;
- and long-term consequences.

## 65.12 Record Integrity

Governance records should be protected against:

- unauthorised deletion;
- silent alteration;
- provenance loss;
- fabricated entries;
- and accidental corruption.

Where corrections are necessary, the system should preserve that a correction occurred.

Correction should not require pretending the earlier record never existed.

A possible record sequence is:

**Original Entry**  
↓  
**Correction**  
↓  
**Reason**  
↓  
**Responsible Authority**  
↓  
**Current Status**

## 65.13 Record Availability

Records should be available to those constitutionally entitled to inspect them.

Availability may vary according to:

- privacy;
- security;
- active investigation;
- personal data;
- commercial confidentiality;
- or other legitimate restrictions.

Restriction should not become disappearance.

Where public access is restricted, appropriate independent oversight should remain possible.

## 65.14 Historical Governance Record

Older Governance decisions remain valuable even after they cease to have legal force.

They provide evidence concerning:

- institutional behaviour;
- precedent;
- policy evolution;
- failed assumptions;
- successful adaptation;
- and constitutional development.

This creates a direct relationship between Governance, Provenance, KCS and civilisational memory.

A superseded decision should therefore normally remain discoverable as:

**SUPERSEDED**

rather than deleted.

## 65.15 Failed Governance Remains Governance Knowledge

Failed decisions should remain preserved.

A failed Governance decision may reveal:

- poor evidence;
- bad incentives;
- weak implementation;
- institutional capture;
- unsuitable context;
- misunderstanding of local conditions;
- or an otherwise reasonable idea whose timing was wrong.

The record should preserve failure without automatically treating it as disgrace.

## 65.16 Record Independence

An institution should not possess unlimited exclusive authority over the records used to judge its own conduct.

Where consequence warrants, records may require:

- independent replication;
- audit access;
- external archival storage;
- distributed preservation;
- or other safeguards.

A self-editable institutional history weakens accountability.

## 65.17 Record and Privacy

Durable records can create privacy risks.

Governance should therefore distinguish:

**what must be preserved**

from

**what must be publicly visible**.

Some material may require:

- redaction;
- restricted access;
- delayed release;
- anonymisation;
- or retention limits.

The constitutional objective is reconstructability with proportionate privacy protection.

## 65.18 Record and AI

AI may increasingly assist:

- indexing;
- search;
- summarisation;
- cross-reference;
- anomaly detection;
- and reconstruction.

AI should not silently alter historical records.

Generated summaries should remain distinguishable from the underlying source.

Where material, AI-derived interpretation should remain traceable.

## 65.19 Governance Record Principle

> **The Governance record exists so that authority, evidence, reasoning, implementation, disagreement, outcome and correction remain reconstructable across time. The record should be sufficiently durable to resist institutional amnesia and sufficiently proportionate that recording itself does not make Governance unusable.**

# Chapter 66 — Proposal Architecture

## 66.1 Proposals as Structured Governance Objects

A proposal should be more than a persuasive statement.

For significant decisions, it should identify enough information for responsible evaluation.

A proposal may contain:

- problem definition;
- desired outcome;
- proposed action;
- constitutional basis;
- affected jurisdictions;
- affected entities;
- expected benefits;
- expected harms;
- evidence;
- alternatives;
- resource requirements;
- implementation;
- risks;
- reversibility;
- dependencies;
- review;
- and success or failure conditions.

The purpose of proposal structure is not to force every idea into unnecessary bureaucracy.

It is to make consequential proposals sufficiently explicit that the relevant Governance process can determine:

- what is actually being proposed;
- why;
- by whom;
- under what authority;
- with what expected consequences;
- and how success or failure will later be recognised.

A poorly structured proposal may conceal disagreement because different participants believe they are deciding upon different things.

Proposal architecture should therefore reduce ambiguity before authority is exercised.

## 66.2 Problem Before Solution

Governance should distinguish:

**What problem are we attempting to solve?**

from:

**What solution has been proposed?**

Institutions can become attached to a proposed mechanism and lose sight of the underlying problem.

If the problem disappears or a better mechanism becomes available, the proposal should be reconsidered.

This distinction also prevents a political or institutional preference from defining the problem entirely around its preferred solution.

For example:

**We need to build Infrastructure X**

is not yet a problem statement.

The underlying problem may instead be:

- insufficient capacity;
- resilience failure;
- geographic inequality;
- poor interoperability;
- high cost;
- or another condition.

Alternative problem definitions may produce entirely different solutions.

## 66.3 Problem Validation

A claimed problem should itself be examined.

Questions include:

- Does the problem exist?
- How large is it?
- Who is affected?
- Is it temporary or persistent?
- Is Governance responsible for it?
- Is intervention likely to improve it?
- Could intervention create greater harm?

Governance should not assume that every politically salient concern requires institutional action.

Problem validation may also ask:

- Is the apparent problem a symptom of another problem?
- Is it local or systemic?
- Is it informational or material?
- Is it caused by incentives?
- Is it a measurement artefact?
- Is it a temporary anomaly?
- Is it produced by another Governance intervention?
- Does addressing the symptom risk preserving the cause?

The quality of the eventual decision depends partly upon the quality of the initial problem definition.

## 66.4 Non-Intervention as an Alternative

The alternatives considered should include, where reasonable:

**Do nothing for now.**

Non-intervention is not automatically negligence.

Sometimes:

- evidence is insufficient;
- the problem may self-correct;
- intervention costs exceed benefits;
- experimentation is preferable;
- or Governance lacks legitimate authority.

Conversely, choosing inaction is still a decision when foreseeable harm follows from delay.

The no-action option should therefore be analysed rather than treated as neutral by default.

It may have:

- costs;
- risks;
- beneficiaries;
- harms;
- path dependencies;
- and irreversibilities

of its own.

## 66.5 Partial Intervention

Governance should consider whether a smaller intervention can achieve most of the desired benefit with lower:

- cost;
- coercion;
- risk;
- complexity;
- or irreversibility.

This follows proportionality.

Partial intervention may include:

- limited jurisdiction;
- narrower eligibility;
- temporary duration;
- reduced authority;
- smaller resource commitment;
- targeted implementation;
- or phased adoption.

The purpose is not always to minimise action.

A partial intervention that cannot plausibly solve the problem may merely delay effective response.

The relevant question is whether a smaller intervention remains competent to achieve the constitutional purpose.

## 66.6 Experimental Intervention

Where uncertainty is high and reversibility is available, a proposal may be converted into a limited experiment.

Instead of:

**Adopt universally.**

Governance may ask:

**Can we test this safely at smaller scale first?**

Experimental intervention may be especially useful where:

- causal assumptions are uncertain;
- behavioural adaptation is difficult to predict;
- implementation is novel;
- technology is rapidly changing;
- local variation is important;
- or failure can be safely contained.

An experiment should not be used where the potential harm is too severe to justify deliberate uncertainty.

## 66.7 Experimental Safeguards

A Governance experiment should identify where appropriate:

- objective;
- hypothesis;
- participants;
- jurisdiction;
- duration;
- evidence baseline;
- authority;
- consent requirements;
- rights protections;
- resources;
- monitoring;
- metrics;
- stop conditions;
- failure conditions;
- review;
- and exit.

The experiment should distinguish:

**testing a mechanism**

from

**quietly establishing a permanent institution under the label of a pilot**.

## 66.8 Sunset Proposal

Where long-term necessity is uncertain, the proposal may contain automatic expiry.

Continuation then requires evidence.

Sunset architecture is particularly useful where:

- authority is novel;
- restrictions are significant;
- evidence is provisional;
- technology is experimental;
- emergency conditions exist;
- or institutional permanence could become difficult to reverse.

Expiry does not prove the mechanism failed.

It creates a scheduled point at which continuation must be reconsidered deliberately.

## 66.9 Pilot Proposal

A pilot should specify:

- population or jurisdiction;
- duration;
- participation conditions;
- baseline;
- expected outcome;
- metrics;
- stop conditions;
- review;
- and what would justify expansion.

A pilot should not be labelled successful merely because it completed.

Completion demonstrates only that the pilot ran.

Success requires comparison between:

- intended outcome;
- observed outcome;
- harms;
- costs;
- unintended effects;
- and relevant alternatives.

## 66.10 Pilot Selection Bias

A pilot may appear successful because it was conducted in unusually favourable conditions.

Examples include:

- highly motivated participants;
- unusually competent administrators;
- high funding;
- strong technical support;
- small scale;
- cooperative communities;
- or intensive oversight unavailable at wider scale.

Expansion should therefore examine whether pilot conditions are reproducible.

## 66.11 Expansion Proposal

A successful local mechanism should not automatically scale universally.

Expansion should consider:

- context dependence;
- scale effects;
- infrastructure requirements;
- cultural variation;
- economic variation;
- population differences;
- and new failure modes introduced by scale.

A mechanism may work at small scale because:

- participants know one another;
- information is local;
- social trust is high;
- coordination is simple;
- or one expert can supervise the entire system.

Those assumptions may disappear at large scale.

## 66.12 Progressive Expansion

Where appropriate, scaling may proceed through stages:

**Pilot**  
↓  
**Replication**  
↓  
**Independent Replication**  
↓  
**Expanded Pilot**  
↓  
**Multi-Context Testing**  
↓  
**Broader Adoption**

This reduces the risk of converting one favourable pilot into universal policy prematurely.

## 66.13 Repeal Proposal

Governance architecture should make it possible to propose removal of:

- laws;
- institutions;
- procedures;
- restrictions;
- programmes;
- and technical systems.

Creation should not be structurally easy while removal is structurally impossible.

A mature Governance system must be capable not only of adding architecture but of removing architecture that has become:

- obsolete;
- harmful;
- redundant;
- captured;
- or unnecessary.

## 66.14 Repeal and Dependency

Repeal may be difficult where people or systems have become dependent upon an existing mechanism.

Governance should therefore distinguish:

**the rule should no longer exist**

from

**the rule can safely disappear immediately**.

Managed repeal may require:

- transition;
- migration;
- replacement infrastructure;
- compensation;
- grandfathering;
- or staged withdrawal.

Dependency should not create permanent immunity.

It may affect the correction pathway.

## 66.15 Simplification Proposal

A valid Governance proposal may exist solely to reduce:

- complexity;
- duplication;
- obsolete procedure;
- reporting burden;
- institutional overlap;
- or unnecessary authority.

Simplification is legitimate constitutional maintenance.

A Governance system can accumulate complexity through individually reasonable additions.

Eventually the combined architecture may become:

- inaccessible;
- contradictory;
- expensive;
- difficult to audit;
- or resistant to citizen participation.

Simplification should therefore be treated as substantive Governance work.

## 66.16 Simplification Is Not Automatic Improvement

Reducing complexity can also remove useful safeguards.

A simplified procedure may become:

- easier to understand;
- faster;
- cheaper;

while also becoming:

- less reviewable;
- more centralised;
- less protective;
- or easier to capture.

Simplification proposals should therefore identify what information or protection is being removed as well as what burden is reduced.

## 66.17 Proposal Provenance

A proposal should preserve where appropriate:

- originator;
- contributors;
- earlier versions;
- evidence sources;
- amendments;
- derived concepts;
- AI contributions;
- and links to prior related proposals.

Provenance allows future reviewers to reconstruct development without turning authorship into authority.

The originator of a proposal does not gain constitutional control over the proposal merely because they originated it.

## 66.18 Proposal Ownership

A proposer does not own the Governance process.

Once a proposal enters legitimate review:

- evidence may alter it;
- amendments may improve it;
- alternatives may replace it;
- or Governance may reject it.

Authorship deserves appropriate provenance.

It does not create a veto over constitutional deliberation.

## 66.19 Proposal Evidence

Evidence attached to a proposal should distinguish where practical:

- observation;
- measured data;
- historical precedent;
- scientific findings;
- expert analysis;
- model output;
- simulation;
- prediction;
- anecdotal evidence;
- affected-party experience;
- and speculation.

Different evidence types serve different purposes.

Their existence should not imply equal reliability.

## 66.20 Proposal Uncertainty

A proposal should identify significant uncertainty where known.

This may include uncertainty concerning:

- costs;
- adoption;
- behavioural response;
- technical performance;
- legal interpretation;
- resource requirements;
- second-order effects;
- or future conditions.

A proposal that hides uncertainty may appear stronger while becoming harder to review honestly.

## 66.21 Proposal Alternatives

A proposal should not define its own alternative as catastrophe unless that claim is supported.

Common false framing may take forms such as:

**Either adopt this proposal or accept failure.**

In reality, there may be:

- alternative mechanisms;
- staged intervention;
- local action;
- delay;
- redesign;
- or no action.

Alternative generation should occur independently enough to avoid proposal capture.

## 66.22 Proposal Success Conditions

Where practical, the proposal should explain what evidence would count as success.

Success conditions may include:

- desired output;
- reduced harm;
- improved resilience;
- cost range;
- participation level;
- rights preservation;
- or other measurable and qualitative criteria.

Success criteria should not be defined entirely by the proposing institution after implementation begins.

## 66.23 Proposal Failure Conditions

The proposal should also identify what would count as unacceptable failure.

Possible conditions include:

- harm threshold;
- cost escalation;
- inability to implement;
- rights violation;
- corruption;
- dependency;
- persistent underperformance;
- or unintended systemic effect.

Failure criteria improve the ability to stop or correct the mechanism.

## 66.24 Proposal Review Conditions

Review may occur:

- after a fixed interval;
- after a threshold;
- after implementation;
- after new evidence;
- after complaint;
- after external change;
- or after another defined trigger.

Review conditions should be proportionate to consequence.

## 66.25 Proposal Architecture Principle

> **A significant Governance proposal should make the problem, proposed intervention, authority, evidence, uncertainty, alternatives, harms, benefits, resource requirements, reversibility, dependencies, implementation, review and failure conditions sufficiently explicit that civilisation can evaluate the proposal rather than merely react to its rhetoric.**

# Chapter 67 — Decision Classification

## 67.1 Why Classification Matters

Different decisions carry different consequences.

A single procedure for every decision would either:

- overburden trivial matters;
- or under-protect important matters.

Governance therefore benefits from decision classification.

Classification helps determine:

- evidence burden;
- deliberation;
- participation;
- review;
- documentation;
- authority;
- and appeal.

Classification should not itself become an instrument for avoiding constitutional scrutiny.

## 67.2 Administrative Decisions

Administrative decisions implement established policy within existing delegated authority.

Examples may include:

- scheduling;
- routine procurement;
- ordinary staffing;
- maintenance;
- and execution of established procedures.

These generally require lighter process.

Administrative authority should not be used to create new policy secretly.

A formally administrative decision may become policy-like where repeated implementation changes practical rights or institutional behaviour.

## 67.3 Operational Decisions

Operational decisions alter how a stewardship function is performed without changing its constitutional purpose.

They may require:

- evidence;
- documentation;
- internal review;
- and performance evaluation.

Examples may include:

- workflow design;
- deployment method;
- operational threshold;
- technical procedure;
- or local service configuration.

Operational decisions can still become constitutionally significant where they materially affect:

- access;
- privacy;
- rights;
- dependency;
- or effective jurisdiction.

## 67.4 Policy Decisions

Policy decisions establish or materially alter a rule, programme or continuing governance approach.

They normally require stronger:

- deliberation;
- evidence;
- alternatives analysis;
- public visibility;
- and review.

A policy decision may affect:

- many people;
- long durations;
- public resources;
- institutional incentives;
- or future precedents.

## 67.5 Cross-Jurisdictional Decisions

These affect multiple legitimate jurisdictions.

They require coordination appropriate to the consequence boundary.

Cross-jurisdictional decisions should identify:

- affected jurisdictions;
- externalities;
- shared infrastructure;
- authority boundaries;
- and escalation path.

The principle remains:

> **The decision field should expand when the consequence field expands.**

## 67.6 Rights-Affecting Decisions

These materially affect constitutional protections.

They require heightened scrutiny.

Relevant questions include:

- Which rights?
- Whose rights?
- How severe is the effect?
- How long does it last?
- Is the effect reversible?
- What remedy exists?
- What authority permits it?
- Is there a less restrictive alternative?

Formal classification should not hide practical rights effects.

## 67.7 High-Irreversibility Decisions

These create consequences difficult or impossible to reverse.

Examples may include:

- destruction of unique environments;
- permanent infrastructure commitments;
- irreversible biological intervention;
- permanent data destruction;
- or certain existential-risk technologies.

High irreversibility should increase scrutiny.

A decision may also be socially or institutionally irreversible even where physical reversal remains possible.

## 67.8 Systemic Decisions

These can materially affect civilisation-wide systems.

Examples may include:

- monetary architecture;
- critical energy systems;
- major AI governance;
- constitutional communications infrastructure;
- or civilisation-wide identity systems.

Systemic decisions require examination of correlated failure.

They should also examine:

- concentration;
- interoperability;
- dependency;
- resilience;
- and recovery.

## 67.9 Constitutional Decisions

These alter:

- foundational authority;
- rights;
- institutional constitutional relationships;
- or constitutional amendment itself.

They require the strongest ordinary process.

Constitutional decisions should not be created through accumulated administrative drift.

Where ordinary decisions collectively alter constitutional reality, Governance should recognise the cumulative effect.

## 67.10 Emergency Decisions

Emergency decisions operate under extraordinary conditions and should be governed by the dedicated emergency architecture.

Emergency classification should not become a route for accelerating ordinary policy.

The emergency purpose, scope and duration should remain visible.

## 67.11 Experimental Decisions

Experimental decisions intentionally create bounded uncertainty to generate evidence.

Their legitimacy depends partly upon:

- bounded consequence;
- informed participation where required;
- reversibility;
- monitoring;
- and stop conditions.

An experiment should not expose unwilling participants to disproportionate constitutional risk merely because the system seeks evidence.

## 67.12 Hybrid Decisions

Some decisions may fit multiple classes.

For example, a policy may be:

- systemic;
